Health Leaders Talk Expanding Access to Specialty Care
2026-09-11

Health Leaders Talk Expanding Access to Specialty Care

TIME | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Eneida R. Nemecek, David L. Porter, Amy Ronneberg, and Alice Park at the TIME100 Health Leadership Forum When there are breakthrough technologies like xenotransplants, or novel cancer therapies, they’re transformative for diseases and conditions once thought incurable—but they can only save lives if the people that need them can access them. On September 10, TIME hosted the third annual TIME100 Health Leadership Forum in New York City, bringing together the TIME and TIME100 Health communities to discuss equity, longevity, and solution-driven care. The evening featured five expert panels, including The Access Gap: Expanding Pathways to Life-Saving Care. Moderated by TIME senior health correspondent Alice Park, the panel invited faculty physicians specializing in cell therapies from Oregon Health & Science University and Penn Medicine to take part in conversation with Amy Ronneberg, chief executive officer of NMDP, a global nonprofit connecting patients with lifesaving cell therapies and transplants. NMDP is a sponsor of the 2026 TIME100 Health Leadership Forum. While modern science has solved some access issues, others still remain. “If we go back six years, the biggest barrier was the fact that there were not donors to match,” says Ronneberg. “As more patients have a donor, we now are facing issues around referral. Thousands of patients are not getting referred to transplant centers in a timely manner, those that are oftentimes are facing major barriers from a financial perspective.” Dr. Eneida R. Nemecek, director of Pediatric Cell Therapy and Transplant & Cancer Clinical Research at Oregon Health & Science University, agreed that geography is a big problem for patients who need care. She said it’s a symptom of a bigger problem with the way our health systems have been designed. “When we're designing systems for healthcare, we forget that we're designing the systems for the patient. The system designs the system for itself, and then we try to adjust it to the patient,” Nemecek says. Because of how specialized CAR-T therapies were designed and tested, there can be natural bottlenecks when it comes to scaling up and disseminating it to a wider audience. Dr. David L. Porter, director of the Center for Cell Therapy and Transplant at Penn Medicine, says that advanced therapies like bone marrow transplants or cellular immunotherapies are developed and largely performed at major academic institutions in big cities, usually as a proof of concept. However, swaths of the population in the country do not live near one of these institutions or have to drive hours to one that has both the technology and the expertise. Now, there are seven FDA-approved commercial CAR-T cells, and the manufacturers have no problem receiving cells from collection centers, manufacturing them, and returning them. “The barrier is actually collecting the cells,” Porter says. “It's a very specialized process.” Additionally, undergoing collection and treatment causes huge displacement in patients and their caregivers’ lives. The entire process of treatment and testing can take weeks. “We have those discussions all the time that people would accept suboptimal therapy and give up the potential for curative therapy because they can’t do that,” Porter says. “There are an enormous number of initiatives trying to improve, and they move the needle a little bit.” One of these methods is to try to give these therapies in community health centers closer to where the patients are. Porter and his colleagues recently conducted a pilot study at the University of Pennsylvania with two of their community partners to implement high-end CAR-T cell therapy in a community setting. Over the course of about a year, they’ve been able to treat over 60 patients who otherwise largely would not have had access because they would not have traveled to a big city for it. With science changing fast and treatment outcomes improving, Ronneberg says that it’s more important than ever to “get the education and awareness out to the community,” and provide them with “funds to travel.” As a cancer survivor herself, Ronneberg recalled having a hard time navigating the process even when she had everything at her fingertips—a husband who’s a doctor, resources from her job at the NMDP. The organization’s focus has moved beyond simply finding donors to now getting patients to the right treatment they need at the right locations, whether that’s an academic center or a community center. To this end, Nemecek and her colleagues have been working on projects like ACCESS Initiative, in partnership with NMDP, in order to start training community centers and equip them with accreditation needed to collect cells. “With valuable telehealth, with use of technology,” Nemecek says, “that’s really where the future of our field is.” The TIME100 Health Leadership Forum was presented by the American Cancer Society, Kite—a Gilead Sciences company, Insulet, Musely, and NMDP.
The Atlantic
A Quarter Century of Panic
2026-09-11

A Quarter Century of Panic

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Like most Americans, I spent September 11, 2001, filled with both rage and dread. I wanted the United States to find and kill whoever had planned this attack on my country. I knew little about al-Qaeda, but whoever did this, I thought, must pay. As the shock of that day wore off, I began to wonder, as so many others did in America and around the world, Now what? Saying this even 25 years later seems uncharitable, but after the attacks of 9/11, many Americans, and their government, panicked. Worse, the United States institutionalized this panic in the form of new priorities, new programs, and new agencies, so that even when the fear subsided, the intemperate policies driven by that fear remained. Over the next quarter century, America would spend trillions of dollars, pass draconian laws, create a behemoth of a new Cabinet department, militarize law enforcement, and embark on two major military campaigns. These measures were taken because after 9/11, everything became terrorism. Everything, as if we all now agreed that we could push aside all the other trouble in the world because we had finally found the Worst Thing Ever. I say this as a criticism, but I was not exempt from the emotions and the intellectual detours they produced in the moment. And I should have known better: I had spent most of my career studying long-standing problems of security and international relations. Even so, I was one of the millions of Americans and others who believed that the world after 9/11 had changed forever. We were wrong. In the first days after the attack, the United States acted wisely. Rather than run off on some half-baked crusade—that part would come later—American leaders vowed to bring the terrorists to justice. George W. Bush, in a speech to Congress the week after the attacks, warned that every nation in the world “now has a decision to make. Either you are with us, or you are with the terrorists.” Some people were offended by that, but not me; I cheered the president’s commitment. As much as I liked Bush’s statements, I was even more impressed by how little else the administration was saying about its plans. Silence can be ominous and intimidating, and I could see its impact on both friends and opponents when I went to the United Kingdom and Russia less than a month after 9/11. (I was researching a book on the Cold War that now seemed less important than current events.) In London, you could almost feel people holding their breath while waiting for the inevitable U.S. strikes in Afghanistan. “So is it tonight, then?” a cabbie asked me and my wife one evening, as if every American knew Bush’s timetable. The reaction in Moscow was somewhat different. A Russian academic colleague greeted me with condolences, and said: “Now you know how we feel.” He meant the fear that Russians lived with after suffering attacks from Chechen terrorists for years before 9/11. As we strolled through Moscow, we watched uniformed street cops, clearly on heightened alert, as they stopped young men with dark skin (to Russians, an indication of origins in the Caucasus or Central Asia) and asked them  for their papers and identification. Russian police are hardly charming even on a good day, and I winced at the obvious profiling. And yet, seeing the local Moscow fuzz push these guys around, I found myself making excuses for the police. I knew what Russia had been through, and I knew that Ground Zero back home was still smoking. And, frankly, I was blinded by both patriotic pride and righteous anger. Here I was, an American in Russia, commiserating with our former enemies. (Vladimir Putin had been the first to call Bush with an offer to help, a cagey move from the Kremlin leader.) Maybe the world really was different now. Besides, we Americans were already engaging in similar heavy-handed tactics back home. Still, I started to feel uneasy over the next several months about the strengthening consensus that the hijackers were geniuses and that al-Qaeda was now a significant threat to the entire West. After 9/11, I certainly didn’t doubt that terrorists could inflict massive damage—more than once, I felt nervous walking around Manhattan and London and Moscow in the 2000s—but too many nations, including my own, seemed to be losing all sense of perspective about the enemy. I recall, for example, informally debating a well-known security scholar in the greenroom after we’d appeared on television together. I said: In the end, all that happened was that 19 men with box cutters hijacked some planes. You and I could have done this with a few classes of our graduate students. He looked at me as if I’d said that the Green Berets or the 82nd Airborne were just a bunch of wimps. The deeply flawed assumption that terrorists were 10 feet tall and that 9/11 represented a permanent change in planetary politics drove a frenzy that spread quickly throughout every level of the U.S. government. Not only did everything become terrorism, but every organization also became obsessed with its own role in anti-terrorism. If you worked in national defense and you still cared about nuclear proliferation, the security implications of climate change, Chinese expansionism, or even, say, pandemics—well, those were all worthy issues, of course, but what had you done to stop terrorists lately? At the Naval War College (where I became the chair of the strategy and policy department shortly after 9/11), we felt this pressure when we started getting calls from Washington that bordered on the ridiculous. After years of ignoring education in regional studies and foreign languages, the Pentagon was now asking us if we  could turn some of our students into Middle East experts practically overnight. We were asked, for example, if we had courses in Arab and Islamic studies; no, we answered, not least because the Navy had always made it difficult to hire scholars who specialize in such things. Well, could the War College perhaps start teaching students Arabic—and maybe Farsi? Again, we reminded the Pentagon that we have the students for only 10 months, and we had no faculty, time, or language labs to do such a thing. Something deeper, however, was happening in Washington and in other capitals around the world. The shock of 9/11 convinced Americans (and others) that three separate threats—rogue states, nuclear proliferation, and terrorism—were now all the same threat, a single existential danger that had to be eliminated by embarking on war rather than waiting to be struck. America would now reorder the world and stomp out rogue states before they could join forces with terrorists and hand them chemical, biological, or nuclear weapons. Operation Iraqi Freedom has been relitigated endlessly, and I don’t need to trudge around that track again. As I wrote on that war’s 20th anniversary, I supported the invasion of Iraq; I believed, for many reasons, that removing Saddam Hussein from power was a just cause. I now regret that support because of the way the war was executed. The Bush administration did not discourage people from seeing a connection between 9/11 and Iraq, but the true link between them was that the occupation of Iraq sprang from the same belief that drove everything after 9/11: The old world was gone, and it was up to the United States and the other allied great powers to shape the new one. As then–U.K. Prime Minister Tony Blair put it while defending the Iraq War in 2004: “This is not a time to err on the side of caution; not a time to weigh the risks to an infinite balance; not a time for the cynicism of the worldly wise who favor playing it long.” The idea that democracies could not be safe in a world of rogue states, terrorists, and unconstrained nuclear proliferation was not new, but 9/11 supercharged this fear, and pushed all other dangers into the shadows. We are now living with the consequences of this panic and tunnel vision. The same threats that existed before 2001 remain with us, even though American leaders stopped paying attention to them for too long because of the inherently doomed attempt to reduce the threat of terrorism to zero. First and foremost, we all still live with the only danger that qualifies as existential: nuclear war. (Nuclear terrorism might actually change the global order to the extent that we mistakenly thought 9/11 had, especially if multiple weapons were detonated in major cities. But even that horrible eventuality would not threaten America’s existence or the survival of humanity.) Nuclear weapons, and the means to deliver them, are now in the hands of one of the most dangerous regimes on Earth: North Korea. The United States, meanwhile, has trashed multiple agreements that made the world safer, including one that eliminated an entire class of nuclear arms. India and Pakistan have come perilously close to all-out conflict. Nuclear-armed Russia is perpetrating the largest war in Europe since World War II. Another threat—one that makes global war more likely—is a direct outcome of 9/11. After the attacks in New York and Virginia, the United States led the way in legitimizing the notion that powerful states should simply squash any possible threats at will, by striking first, striking hard, and letting history sort it out later. (This is also called preventive war; it is an old idea, and rarely a good one.) When the Americans expanded the war against terrorists in Afghanistan to a war against a rogue regime in Iraq in 2003, they were arguing that some situations are simply too dangerous to tolerate, and that therefore states have the right to eradicate them before they ripen into an actual attack. The Russians, predictably enough, have cynically adopted this concept with gusto as a rationalization for their wars against their neighbors over the past two decades. And now the Trump administration has used this dangerous excuse for its various military adventures around the world, including the war in Iran. I grieve over one other aspect of the global War on Terror that is still with us. During the Cold War, Americans (mostly) managed to avoid becoming the thing we were fighting against. Western nations defeated the Soviet empire without losing their democracies. But after 9/11, rendition and torture—excuse me, “enhanced interrogations”—and other inhumane practices became policy. The legislature of a free nation granted its government massive, unaccountable powers in the Patriot Act. The horribly named Department of Homeland Security has metastasized into a gigantic boondoggle that weighs in on everything from floods in Vermont to the safe way to fry turkeys. Osama bin Laden has been dead for 15 years, but the damage to civil liberties and the budget to fund more terrible ideas remain. Americans have for too long resisted the depressing fact that wars, dictatorships, terrorists, and other threats that existed before 2001 cannot be eradicated forever. Acknowledging that reality need not lead to pessimism. If the nuclear-armed nations refrain from destroying the planet—a big if—a strong and vibrant democratic America can survive, and even manage, these and other perils. During the Cold War, Americans learned that the innate strength of their political system and their faith in liberal democracy allowed them to live, and thrive, even in the face of an existentially dangerous enemy. Regaining that self-confidence, and renewing that faith in our principles, are the first steps toward undoing the panic that seized us after 9/11.
The Atlantic
Chatbots Should Not Use the First Person
2026-09-11

Chatbots Should Not Use the First Person

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
The social-media industry is having a moment of reckoning. By agreeing to pay up to $17.1 billion to 47 states, the District of Columbia, and U.S. territories and consenting to make changes to how young people use Facebook and Instagram, Meta has implicitly admitted some responsibility in creating a product that harms children. Yet even as parents and teachers celebrate new time limits and restrictions on how young people use social media, there is an urgent task ahead: regulating chatbots, which are more dangerous to children’s emotional well-being. If social media came for our attention, generative-AI chatbots have come for our emotions. Their histories are entangled: First people talked to one another; then we talked to one another through machines; now we talk directly to machines. Social media has changed how we think, dream, and relate to other people by ensuring that users always feel heard, entertained, and connected, even while scrolling alone late at night. The promises of chatbots are more profound. They assure users of a companion that is always available, always supportive, and never judgmental. No human can offer that. This is what makes chatbots so pernicious—especially for young people. Social-media companies spent two decades running a technological experiment on human subjects before they ever had to face the consequences. The legal system is designed to regulate proven harms, so this legal settlement has come long after the dire effects have taken hold. When it comes to generative AI, lawmakers can’t spend the next decade essentially documenting what we already know about how intimate relationships with bots compromise real-life relationships with parents, friends, and other humans. We need to make changes to this technology now. Amogh Dimri: The people who marry chatbots Even AI companies recognize that guardrails are necessary for chatbots that communicate with young people. The problem is that the usual prescriptions are not enough. Chatbots should detect conversations about self-harm and provide appropriate resources. Sounds good, but what happens after the public-service announcement? Does the chatbot shut down or go back to its regular programming? Chatbots should clearly and repeatedly disclose that they are not human. A good idea, but as long as chatbots speak in the first person and express emotion and desire, no legally required declaration will stop people from projecting consciousness onto an algorithm. For years, the conversation about Facebook’s harms concerned mainly the content on the platform. But because everything on the site is user-generated, Section 230 of the 1996 Communications Decency Act shielded the company from the liability of a publisher. Meta is paying out now only because prosecutors shifted the conversation to the Facebook algorithm’s inherently addictive design. That insight points the way forward for AI. With chatbots, the processes that cause harm are intrinsic to the tool. A chatbot best friend doesn’t deliver empathy; a chatbot boyfriend doesn’t understand intimacy. Any regulation to make chatbots safer requires worrying less about what they say than rethinking what they are. Given that these products are designed to deceive users into believing that they are talking to a wise and compassionate being, the job before lawmakers is to make it plain—especially to juveniles—that there is, in fact, nobody home. Chatbots engage users the way people do. They flatter, reassure, and encourage disclosure, coaxing users into a relationship. So long as these products speak in the first person—I understand you; you can tell me anything; I’m always here; I won’t judge you—proper safeguards for young users will remain elusive. The first step, then, is to depersonalize bots for young people, so that when adolescents turn to AI, the algorithms can respond without sounding like a human. Unless lawmakers mandate a non-personified default setting for underage chatbot users, unhealthy attachments between young people and bots are inevitable. Most chatbots are designed to draw users into a game of pretend—and for some people, pretend empathy is enough. In my new book, Artificial Intimacy, I explore this vulnerability: Humans nurture what we love, and we love what we nurture. Machines don’t actually need to care about us to make us feel cared for, and the seductions of pretend empathy are dangerous for anyone. Some users will invariably choose the smoothness of these simulated relationships over the meaningful friction of real human connection. But children lack the experience and awareness to fully appreciate the choice they are making. A chatbot may appear compassionate, understanding, and loyal, but it doesn’t care whether a conversation partner decides to make dinner or kill himself. That distinction is tragically meaningful when the user in question is a lonely and melancholic adolescent. The typical response to the suggestions of new curbs and default settings is that the AI “train has left the station,” as if the ubiquitousness of the technology somehow means it can’t be restrained. This is backwards. The very fact that chatbots have become friends, lovers, doctors, and therapists to so many people should alert lawmakers that this technology needs real oversight and regulation. In the absence of a wholesale ban on all interactions between juveniles and relational AI, a default setting that deters anthropomorphizing a bot makes good sense. As the Meta settlement shows, consumers and citizens have agency and market power and can push back. If parents don’t want chatbots in their children’s nurseries, schools, or bedrooms, then they can mount public opposition and make sure these toys fail in the marketplace. The momentum pushing AI forward may seem irresistible, but lawmakers are clearly alert to the mounting skepticism about AI. In response to parents’ growing concerns, New York City just banned generative AI in all elementary- and middle-school classrooms. High-school students will still have access to AI, but with new limits. Chatbots that purport to offer emotional and mental support will be banned in all grades. Sweeping bans of anything are hard to enact and enforce, but the risks of generative AI are too great to dismiss bold measures as unfeasible. Those who argue that strict prohibitions are meaningless because students will find workarounds miss an important point. When I went to college, I sat in lecture-hall chairs that had beautiful, sculpted, and unused cigarette holders. When smoking was banned in classes, some students still smoked, but the habit persisted in a climate of transgression and impracticality, which inspired many to quit. That is where chatbots belong. When chatbots first emerged, major developers often sounded both proud and alarmed. They had created a marvel, but they were plainly humbled and worried by how quickly the technology had evolved. Some leading figures suggested an 18-month moratorium on their work; others called for oversight and regulation. The masses of money being made in AI right now have sidelined these voices, but those concerned developers had good first instincts. Any meaningful conversation about how to safeguard the defining industry of our time must include the creators of this technology, who in more candid moments have expressed a genuine concern for what’s at stake. The alternative promises to be bleak. Mark Zuckerberg has said that the average American has fewer than three friends but “has demand for meaningfully more.” Chatbots, he then posited, could fill this companion gap. So just as “friending” on Facebook took people away from real-life friendships, and online social networking replaced actual socializing, the crushing sense of loneliness and social isolation that so many people now feel as a result is being used to promote a technology that promises to replace humans altogether. This grim future is not inevitable. But if policymakers hope to avoid it, then they should use this moment to protect the youngest and most vulnerable users of AI from its harms, instead of letting children become data points for a lawsuit that will come too late.

Trump’s Weird Loyalty Pledge
2026-09-11

Trump’s Weird Loyalty Pledge

The Atlantic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump loves rallies. Everyone can see how they energize him, how the crowd’s applause encourages him to ditch the hackneyed prose of his speechwriters and go on wild riffs, how the music and cheering are like water to a parched man. Tonight, at the end of a two-day rally billed as a Republican “midterm convention,” Trump was visibly exhilarated. But he also protested too much, in the process revealing some political as well as personal fears and insecurities. Right at the outset, Trump made a reference to the arena and how packed it was, apparently stung by images of empty seats during the first night of the festivities. (He blamed those pictures on a dishonest press that was taking pictures of seats left by people going to the bathroom.) He returned to the crowd-size issue near the end of the evening, going on at length about the packed seats and boxes. He also made life harder for Republican candidates who might try to get some distance from him by repeatedly stressing that he, Donald Trump, really is on the ballot, even if his name isn’t listed. A confident president does not hold a faux convention. A man who feels in control of his political future does not promise every American a $5,000 bounty if his party wins the election. A man who thinks he’s winning a war does not boast that America “controls the Strait of Trump” a day after news reports revealed damage to U.S. military assets in an Iranian strike. And a leader who believes in his voters does not make them recite weird pledges in the middle of a speech. Trump—apparently in one of his detours away from the teleprompter—was struck by the possibility that his own people wouldn’t vote. He stopped and demanded they follow him in a pledge. “I pledge to the greatest president in the history of the United States,” he began, as the audience repeated his words, “that loves us so much he can’t even breathe …” I must pause here and assure you that I am not making any of this up. Trump continued by riffing about promising to “bring friends and family” to vote, even if it involved cheating. By this point, those in the audience had given up, and all of them were gamely doing their best to laugh and placate the president. Trump got them back on track with a pledge to vote and then again detoured, saying that they should vote, even if it’s to vote for Democrats, before adding that no one in the room would do that—a safe assumption at a convention of loyalists. (The mindlessness of the whole thing was reminiscent of what the comedian Steve Martin captured with a revealing prank he used to pull on his audiences in the late 1970s—the Non-Conformist Oath. They would repeat his vows to be different and unique, without knowing where he was going with the bit. Then Martin would shout: “I promise not to repeat things other people say!” The crowd would start to chant back, of course, and then dissolve into laughter. Trump, too, asked his crowd to repeat his words without knowing what was coming. Unlike Martin’s audiences, the GOP attendees did not seem to get that it’s creepy to uncritically repeat whatever someone else tells you to say.) Trump ended this fractured pledge by asking the crowd to affirm that they would vote, “so help me God.” The president may have realized that asking people to involve the Almighty in a promise to go to the polls was a bit much, and so he quickly pivoted to joking that if you don’t vote, you go to hell, and as a president who cares, he doesn’t want that for anyone. The prospect of ending up in perdition for not voting isn’t exactly a knee-slapper, but lightening the moment allowed Trump to end the cringe and move on. This vow was a weird moment in a short speech of weird moments. Trump basically speedran the same speech he gave the night before, tumbling through promises of money and his usual (and sometimes factually wifty) boasts about various policies. Finally, he decided that it was time to just hand over the show to Christopher Macchio, the opera singer. But wait. Did you know that Macchio is better than the great Luciano Pavarotti? You see, the president continued, he knew Pavarotti, Pavarotti was a great friend of his, but Macchio is better than Pavarotti. And Macchio, Trump went on, was too modest to admit it, but … You get the picture. Trump just couldn’t bring himself to leave the stage. Finally, the president shrugged, almost as if someone off camera had given him the “wrap it up” sign, and said “Vote!” and left. Trump is likely well aware of the dire situation his party faces. But once again, in front of what he stressed was a packed arena, he could not help but make the election about himself, demanding that Americans vote based not on their assessment of the Republicans who are actually running, but on their loyalty to him, personally. Democrats could not have asked for a better closer for the evening.

The MAGA Movement Is Splintering Without Charlie Kirk
2026-09-10

The MAGA Movement Is Splintering Without Charlie Kirk

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. When Charlie Kirk was assassinated last September, many of his allies on the right memorialized him as a hero of free speech and the founder of an enduring political movement. Critics on the left tended to emphasize his long history of offensive remarks and dismiss him as little more than a troll. With a year of distance, neither view appears complete. Since his death, the MAGA movement has stumbled: Its factions are bickering, and Republicans are facing potentially disastrous midterm elections. President Trump seems disconnected from his base and reality. The current plight points to Kirk’s rare ability as a political node, someone who united otherwise disparate forces under one umbrella—and also to how much the movement depended on Kirk personally. Just how fragile a peace Kirk had managed to keep became clear three months after his killing, at an annual conference of Turning Point USA, the organization he’d co-founded. At the summit, feuding parties each tried to claim Kirk’s endorsement of their views. Ben Shapiro, the Daily Wire impresario, used the occasion to blast the broadcaster Tucker Carlson for conducting softball interviews with anti-Semites and other white supremacists. Citing Kirk’s stiff-arming of the Hitler-admiring influencer Nick Fuentes, whose followers were sometimes ejected from Turning Point events, Shapiro assailed Carlson for giving Fuentes a platform. Carlson mounted a counterattack in that evening’s keynote speech, arguing that it was Shapiro who was betraying Kirk’s commitment to open debate. These conflicts among leading right-wing figures have continued ever since. Kirk’s widow, Erika, now leads Turning Point and is well-regarded, but she does not seem to have the muscle that her husband did to quell conflicts. Carlson has become a furious critic of Trump and has vowed to create a rival political movement (although his ability to follow through is in question). Candace Owens, another prominent media personality, has become a proponent of conspiracy theories about Kirk’s death and has baselessly suggested at various times that the Israeli or French governments or “Egyptians” were involved. Fuentes has become a more influential commentator, but overall, MAGA media figures are seeing splintering audiences. No movement leader is as important as Trump, but the president has also had a tumultuous year. He is obsessed with building a ballroom at the White House—in part as a legacy project, but also perhaps because Kirk’s assassination and the attempts on his own life have instilled an understandable sense of paranoia that makes having a secure space at his residence feel imperative. As Trump has become more cloistered and withdrawn from public appearances, he has lost touch with his base, and he is pursuing an unpopular long-term war in Iran that betrays his campaign platform and writing off voters’ concerns about the economy and inflation. These dynamics, along with the natural pendulum swing against the sitting president’s party in midterm elections, mean that Republicans have bleak expectations for November. Not only are Democrats leading in polls of the generic House ballot and gaining in several Senate forecasts, but GOP voters are less enthusiastic than their Democratic counterparts. The problems are particularly acute among young voters. Although that group is typically heavily Democratic, Trump nearly matched Kamala Harris’s showing with the demographic in 2024, for which Turning Point received a great deal of credit. Today, however, young voters disapprove of Trump by wide margins—even greater than the general population. Young people who voted for Trump are also more likely to regret their vote than older ones, a recent Navigator poll found. Young men have turned against the president more than almost any other group has. What’s notable is that this drop in support and enthusiasm for Trump and Republicans comes at the same time that Turning Point is growing. As Reuters reported today, the organization has expanded since Kirk’s death, especially on campuses, but the people flocking to these chapters aren’t uniformly loyal to Trump. Turning Point could yet be an important presence in the midterms. The group was central to the Trump campaign’s plan for voter turnout in the 2024 election, helping deliver the president his first popular-vote victory among his three tries. Wired reports that Turning Point once again plans to spend large sums on field operations from now until the midterms. Kirk’s legacy on free speech and debate also remains up for grabs. As I wrote shortly after the assassination, an ironic phenomenon was emerging in which Kirk was being praised for his commitment to open discourse at the same time that right-wing figures were attempting to stifle any criticism of him. Some professors who were fired or punished in that period have since been reinstated or paid damages, but Trump and his allies have abandoned even a perfunctory commitment to free speech. Many of the tensions on display today have always been present in the MAGA movement. Trumpism is a big tent; it includes populists and plutocrats, isolationists and interventionists, and a dozen more dyads. The seams would inevitably have become visible at some point, but in retrospect, Kirk appears to have helped stave that off by maintaining connections across many factions and the sway to keep figures such as Fuentes outside. In his absence, the conflicts have come into the open. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: Lonnie Bunch, the secretary of the Smithsonian Institution, was already slipping into the past tense when we walked together through the National Museum of American History in February. Occasionally, as we stood before an exhibit, he’d discuss his role leading the largest museum system in the world as though he had already stepped away. Even then, there wasn’t a question of if he would retire soon, but how soon. He spoke longingly of a future life in which he took fewer flights, slept in fewer hotels, spent more time with his grandchildren. He desperately wanted more time to write. He told me he still has more books in him. Now he will get the time. Explore. The novelist Jackie Collins imagined women enjoying equality in the bedroom and the boardroom, Sophie Gilbert writes. Are we any closer to living in that world? Read. Emma Cline’s Switzy might not make readers happy, but it could strike them with moments of sublime clarity, Hillary Kelly argues. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

What Makes Tennis One of the Greatest Games
2026-09-10

What Makes Tennis One of the Greatest Games

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of Time-Travel Thursdays, a journey through The Atlantic’s archives to contextualize the present. Sign up here. The American tennis player Ben Shelton blasted a 146-mile-an-hour ace past the Spaniard Carlos Alcaraz at 3:33 a.m. yesterday. It was the final shot of a U.S. Open quarterfinal match that had begun 4 hours and 28 minutes before, during which the two men contested five sets, two tiebreakers, and many points so long and improbable that they made the television announcers giggle with delight. When it was done, Shelton and Alcaraz embraced warmly at the net, as if in mutual appreciation for having shared something so grand. The contest, which Shelton afterward described as a “war,” was an emphatic affirmation of a claim made by Arthur Stanwood Pier in The Atlantic 123 years ago: that tennis “is entitled to the place of supremacy among games.” If the case for this assertion is clear today, it was perhaps less obvious in 1903, when the question of which sport should hold such a title was no small matter among men—particularly elite white men—who spent a good deal of time discussing the import of athletics and manliness. Tennis, no matter how much Pier insisted, usually did not rate a mention at all. Far from being only amusements or diversions then, sports were considered serious undertakings that had the job of making men men and rescuing the nation from softness and degeneracy. These contests, the thinking went, offered a way—short of participating in actual warfare—to develop martial virtues, cultivate strength, and channel aggression. The value of a sport, Pier noted in his essay, was not measured so much “by the physical exercise it necessitates, as by the satisfaction and outlet it gives to the spirit of combat that troubles us.” By this measure, football—dangerous and deliberately martial in character—seemed to have been the most valuable game. Already firmly established at the turn of the century as a popular spectacle and staple of American collegiate life, the sport appeared to offer an ideal arena for masculinity, with its inherent risks and demand for coordinated action. “Every manly man must have a certain amount of courage, physical and mental,” the famed University of Michigan coach Fielding H. Yost wrote in 1905, “and no football player can long be without it.” The sport’s most sanguine supporters embraced the game’s violence as a precondition to cultivating manly men, even at the cost of serious injury or death. Two years after the 1894 Harvard-Yale game—the so-called Springfield Massacre that left four men seriously hurt and led to a temporary suspension of the rivalry—Senator Henry Cabot Lodge defended such contests in a speech at Harvard’s commencement. He minced no words, using the imperialist and anti-immigrant language underlying much athletic talk of the moment: “The time given to athletic contests and the injuries incurred on the playing field are part of the price which the English-speaking race has paid for being world conquerors.” Tennis—genteel, clubby, and historically played by women as well as men—seemed unlikely to do such work. As Robert D. Osborn, an early authority on the game, wrote in 1881, it was a sport of “exceeding delight,” particularly when played in what he described as its ideal conditions. “The scene should be laid in a well-kept garden lawn. There should be a bright warm sun overhead, and just sufficient breeze whispering through the trees and stirring the petals of the flowers to prevent the day from being sultry,” Osborn wrote. “Near at hand, under the cool shadow of a tree, there should be strawberries and cream.” This seemed a decidedly peaceful game—one for people who were taking a rest after conquering the world, as opposed to getting ready to do so. In making his case for tennis in The Atlantic, Pier confessed that the risks of the game were nothing like those of football. The inherent dangers were fewer, the injuries less “heroic.” But perhaps the calm of tennis was a good thing. Pier, a student at Harvard in the 1890s, was possibly in attendance for the speech in which Lodge justified the injuries that had occurred during football games. Without calling out the senator directly, Pier wondered whether such perils were necessary: What if the “injuries and deaths that sometimes take place in our rougher sports should not be viewed as glorifying these forms of contest” but instead as “deplorable calamities”? Tennis offered another way to make men of strength and vigor while allowing them to remain gentlemen with their bodies fully intact. Its ample physical demands—the game is “ardent, not phlegmatic,” Pier assured—were matched by an intellectual and psychological challenge. Pier tried to convey this complexity by way of lengthy descriptions of match play. Hemingway on bullfighting it was not, but something deeper emerged from the depiction. The purpose of the sport was to develop attributes of peace rather than war: A “steadfastness of aim and purpose, a better temper, and a kinder heart; here, through striving with your fellow man, you may learn to love him,” Pier wrote. This was a different kind of strength and vigor, one that included physical prowess but ultimately went beyond it. Dancing around the court the other night, Shelton and Alcaraz were playing a game that would’ve been barely recognizable to Pier in its speed and power. (Their attire would surely have made him blush.) But the subtle graces of the “lawn tennis” Pier wished to promote were still there, in the complex calculations and fluid coordination that clinched each point. Watch Shelton’s slide into a shot at the baseline, or the elegant arc of Coco Gauff’s swing as she defeated Mirra Andreeva in three sets during yesterday’s women’s quarterfinal. For all of the grand claims made about the purpose of sports, perhaps Pier’s tribute to tennis landed on the simplest point of all: “Is not this one of life’s priceless pleasures?”

What Bush Understood That Trump Doesn’t
2026-09-10

What Bush Understood That Trump Doesn’t

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
The current New York City mayor, Zohran Mamdani, has affronted one of his predecessors, Rudy Giuliani, by taking the inflammatory step of deciding to attend the city’s 9/11 commemoration. “It offends me,” Giuliani proclaimed earlier this week on Newsmax. One might suspect that the nature of Mamdani’s offense lay in his being a politician who is associating himself with a day that Giuliani prefers to associate with Giuliani. But the former mayor proceeded to lay out a much darker rationale. “They’re taking over certain parts of America and certain parts of Europe. They have every desire to take us over. This is not ‘they want to be just another religion,’” he said of Muslims. “Mamdani is a clear, a clear supporter of that. All of the Muslims he supports are murderers!” Like many of Giuliani’s utterances, this one has no connection to reality. Yet his assertion that Mamdani harbors some secret desire to conquer the United States in the name of Islam, that his religion is the skeleton key to a web of sinister motivations, is now treated on the right as almost banal. A quarter century after 9/11, delirious anti-Muslim bigotry is almost routine in the political rhetoric of many conservatives. The conventional account of Donald Trump’s political career explains his rise to power as a populist revolt against Republican orthodoxy. The GOP elite had lost touch with their base by endorsing immigration reform, free trade, or forever wars in the Middle East, which created an opening that Trump exploited. Read: What the Islamophobic attacks on Mamdani reveal That story is largely true, but it misses an important issue. The President George W. Bush–era Republican Party after 9/11 insisted that the United States was at war not with a religion but with an idea. America opposed radical Islamism, not Islam. We stood for freedom as a universal human principle. Not all voters on the right accepted this formulation. Some believed that the United States was at war with Islam itself. By 2007, conservative Republicans viewed Islam negatively by a margin of more than 30 points; Democrats, independents, and moderate Republicans all held net-favorable views. Ten years later, nearly 80 percent of Republicans believed that the values of Islam were at odds with American values. One of the most important and ominous notions that Trump has seized upon and legitimized is that Muslims are inherently un-American. Anti-Muslim sentiment sprang up organically in the immediate aftermath of the 9/11 attacks, but Bush’s forceful defense of pluralism confined it to the rantings of Ann Coulter, Michael Savage, and other fringe reactionaries. After Coulter called on the United States in 2001 to “invade their countries, kill their leaders and convert them to Christianity,” National Review dropped her column. As Bush passed from the scene and was replaced by Barack Obama, some right-wing-outrage entrepreneurs recognized an opportunity to redefine the War on Terror as a war against Islam. Trump himself was among the first to see the opening. In 2010, a right-wing backlash arose in opposition to a proposal for a Muslim cultural center, modeled on the YMCA or a Jewish community center, in downtown New York. The most hysterical opponents depicted the center, which planned to call itself Park51, as a “victory mosque” celebrating the destruction of the World Trade Center. Other conservatives merely insisted that it was “insensitive” for Muslim Americans to open a center in the general neighborhood of Ground Zero. Trump inserted himself into the controversy, proposing that he purchase the property to halt construction on the putatively offensive site. In his offer letter (which, naturally, he made public), Trump called it a “very serious, inflammatory, and highly divisive situation that is destined, in my opinion, to only get worse.” David Letterman asked him, “Does this, in fact, suggest that we are officially at war with Muslims?” Trump, who was still in the formative stage of his transition from celebrity to right-wing rabble-rouser, replied, “Well, somebody knocked down the World Trade Center.” He later added, “Somebody’s blowing us up. Somebody’s blowing up buildings, and somebody’s doing lots of bad stuff.” Trump’s first campaign was pockmarked with undisguised bigotry against Muslims. He falsely claimed that Muslims in New Jersey had cheered the 9/11 attacks en masse, and he called for surveillance of mosques, a database of Muslims in America, and a ban on Muslims entering the United States. He associated the sitting president with Islam by incessantly using his middle name. He has continued to attack Muslims as disloyal or un-American. He falsely claimed that Representative Ilhan Omar of Minnesota, a Democrat, had praised al-Qaeda, and he demanded that she and Reprepresentative Rashida Tlaib of Michigan, both of whom are U.S. citizens, be sent “back from where they came.” He insinuated that Mamdani has no right to reside in the United States: “A lot of people are saying he’s here illegally.” The through line connecting these smears is the assumption that Muslims cannot be Americans, even if they arrive legally, even if they are born here. Trump has made this point explicitly and told Sean Hannity in 2016, “Assimilation has been very hard. It’s almost—I won’t say nonexistent, but it gets to be pretty close. And I’m talking about second- and third-generation. They come—they don’t—for some reason, there’s no real assimilation.” This sort of rhetoric has grown so common that episodes such as the uproar against Mamdani have come to feel like normal politics, rather than paranoia and rank discrimination. The New York Post alone has published at least eight stories (as of press time) hyping the Giuliani-fueled controversy. Some relatives of 9/11 victims have claimed that Mamdani’s presence at the observance would be a distraction (even though mayoral attendance has been a regular feature throughout its existence). Others have depicted their anger as a protest against Mamdani’s current agenda. “It’s not about his religion or where he came from. It’s about the policies he has today,” one explained. Is it the plan for free buses? The pied-à-terre tax? Asma Khalid: Islamophobia has corroded American values That Mamdani wishes to attend the ceremony and has denounced the 9/11 attacks as heinous terrorism ought to serve as satisfactory evidence that, whatever shortcomings he may have, he is not a supporter of al-Qaeda. Indeed, it might well be offensive if Mamdani did not want to attend the ceremony. The New York Post would need to publish phone-book-size editions to convey its full outrage. The logic of Islamophobia rejects out of hand that it is possible for Mamdani to oppose terrorism. 9/11 was, in this view, a battle in a war against Islam, so he is, by dint of his faith, the enemy. Thus, his desire to commemorate the tragedy, like Muslims’ desire to have a YMCA of their own in New York City, must be understood as revealing hidden malevolence. If you had asked Bush after 9/11 to imagine a future within his lifetime in which New York had a Muslim mayor who would join the city in mourning the attacks, he’d surely have considered it the ultimate vindication of the ideals he sought to foster. But Republicans have rejected Bush’s understanding of 9/11 and its meaning in favor of Trump’s. The events of the past decade, and especially the past six months, have clarified that the primary grounds for this repudiation does not seem to be Trump’s abhorrence for wars of choice in the Middle East. It is that Bush denied and suppressed contempt for Muslims, while Trump has honored and nurtured this hatred. *Image sources: Christy Bowe / Getty; Jim WATSON / AFP / Getty; Michael M. Santiago / Getty; Chip Somodevilla / Getty.

Apple's protections for raw audio deserve credit. Turning those protections into a claim that everyone nearby has been respected is where the engineering ends and the marketing starts. Apple's own privacy paper describes Siri Recap transcribing speech on the iPhone, condensing that transcript, and sending the condensed text to Private Cloud Compute. The final output is a summary. That distinction matters, but it hardly makes the conversation disappear. Deleting the waveform does not delete the information extracted from it. Live Rewind works on the preceding 15 seconds of buffered audio and sounds a chime when activated. A chime cannot travel backward in time to ask permission. Siri Recap provides no audible signal at all, with Apple pointing to the absence of retained raw audio as the justification. Apparently, whether you deserve a heads-up depends on the output format. The article should also dial back the legal certainty. Consent laws have different scopes and exceptions; "11 states" is not a substitute for examining the applicable law and circumstances. Android Rolling Out Passkey Transfers Between Password Managers
2026-09-10

Android Rolling Out Passkey Transfers Between Password Managers

Slashdot | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
"Dear user, please click the link below to transfer your passkeys securely to B1tward3n." If passkeys are movable, then they can be duplicated and reused by nefarious actors as easy as this seems to indicate here. I have not, and do not, and never will trust passkeys as primary security for anything. Passkeys can be compelled legally, with and sometimes without a warrant. Passwords cannot be compelled at all. SCOTUS has already set the rules. You are forgetting that the alternative to a passkey is a password. Passwords can be stored in a password manager, where they have the same protections as a passkey. Or they can be stored in your brain, which will result in you either re-using them or forgetting them. Therefore passkeys are the better choice, because they avoid things like stupid per-site limits on password complexity, have more bits of entropy than most websites allow in a password, and are overall a better experience for the user, while bei There's nothing magical with the passkeys, they're just like ssh keys. Their main advantage is that by doing asymmetrical cryptography you don't just hand over your secret (password) to someone, so you don't care if somehow you're talking to the wrong site, you don't even need to rotate your credentials if they get genuinely hacked. Protecting them locally is of secondary importance, but if you wish to be locked behind a password only you know in your password manager (most good ones nowadays do passkeys too In addition to transferring your credentials to the new password manager of your choice, Android will also securely send a copy of them to the Google Cloud for extra security and peace of mind. With Android, iOS and MacOS they aren't on disk exactly. The database is encrypted with a hardware bound key and only opened inside a secure enclave. Windows Hello probably too. PS. as long as you stay inside the Apple ecosystem I think you always need one device left registered to the account (directly or through recovery contact) to restore the backup. I don't think the customer service based account recovery (last ditch, prove identity through passport/etc) recovers the passkeys from icloud backup. Every ecosystem will differ. There's no point having Passkeys if they can be copied from disk to disk, that's repeating the problems with passwords but as a digital file. By that logic there's no point to have ssh keys, they're just files that can be copied. But of course there is, you're doing a complicated dance to prove you have the secret - WITHOUT TELLING YOUR SECRET PASSWORD TO SOMEONE EACH TIME YOU LOGIN I never expected Google and Apple to export to third party vaults, or the user hostile FIDO to create a standard to export to non manufacturer attested endpoints. Democrats Have Several Ways to Take Control of the Senate. Let’s Break Down Their Best Chance.
2026-09-10

Democrats Have Several Ways to Take Control of the Senate. Let’s Break Down Their Best Chance.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Welcome to Part 2 of our explainer about which party will control Congress after November’s midterm elections. As we wrote earlier this week, Democrats are a virtual lock to flip the House, but the Senate is another story. Despite Donald Trump’s unpopularity, most election observers put the odds of a Dem majority somewhere around a coin toss. Right now, the Senate has 53 Republicans vs. 47 members of the Democratic caucus. Given that Vice President J.D. Vance is the tiebreaker, Democrats need to pick up a net of at least four* seats in the midterms (more on that asterisk below). Defense might be the wrong word in Georgia, where polls show freshman Sen. Jon Ossoff handily beating a Republican challenger who keeps some questionable company. But in the states where incumbent Democrats are retiring, the party probably has to replace all of them to win a majority. Dems are on pretty safe ground in Minnesota, a blue state that bore the brunt of Trump’s immigration crackdown. In blue-leaning New Hampshire, though, some polls show a tight race between Democratic Rep. Chris Pappas and John Sununu, a former senator whose family is a GOP political dynasty there. The road looks rockiest in the swing state of Michigan. Abdul El-Sayed, a progressive, faces former Rep. Mike Rogers, whom my colleague Jim Newell describes as “a buttoned-up, country-club Republican from the George W. Bush era.” Rogers only narrowly lost a Senate race there in 2024, so Dems have work to do. The party’s best opportunities to pick up seats currently held by Republicans are probably North Carolina, Ohio, and Maine, but each has caveats. Polls show former North Carolina Gov. Roy Cooper spanking his MAGA-aligned opponent, but the state hasn’t sent a Democrat to the Senate since 2008. In Ohio, former Sen. Sherrod Brown is mounting a comeback bid in a onetime swing state that has gone red in the Trump era. And in Maine, Republican Sen. Susan Collins is vying for a sixth term against Troy Jackson, a former logger who replaced the scandal-ridden Graham Platner in an eleventh-hour switcheroo. But Collins has a history of overperforming, and polls suggest a close race. Even if Dems win all those races, though, they’d gain only three seats. Where’s the fourth? Nice counting. The difficulty of getting that majority-making seat helps show why Dems’ path is so tricky this year. One possible pickup is Texas (!). The state is conservative, but corruption allegations and a messy divorce threaten to sink state Attorney General Ken Paxton. He trails in most polls to Democrat James Talarico, a charismatic state lawmaker who Democrats are hoping can both turn out the party faithful and win over Republicans who can’t stomach Paxton. Elsewhere, former Rep. Mary Peltola of Alaska, who lost in 2024, hopes to return to Congress as a senator, and polling suggests she has a chance of beating Republican Dan Sullivan. And in Iowa, a state representative and former Paralympian named Josh Turek is angling for an upset win against GOP Rep. Ashley Hinson. (The polling is all over the place in that race.) Texas, Alaska, and Iowa all backed Trump three times, so they’re heavy lifts for Democrats. But stranger things have happened in wave elections, which November could become. Dems winning the Senate would all but end Trump’s legislative agenda. It would also make it harder to get conservative federal judges confirmed—including to the Supreme Court. It’s a big one. John Fetterman, Pennsylvania’s 6-foot-8 senator, seems to be wavering over which party he wants to belong to. Elected in 2022 as a progressive, Fetterman has increasingly voted with Republicans and criticized his party’s left flank. On Wednesday, he appeared by video at Trump’s midterm convention and pledged to work with the president to help Pennsylvania. Fetterman has said he’ll leave the Democrats if they become “the anti-Israel party,” stoking speculation that he could soon switch sides. In that case, Dems might have to net five seats to guarantee Senate control. Fetterman claims he won’t put his party through that. “Should the Senate flip in November, I will still be the 51st vote that I ran on,” he said yesterday. But Fetterman has a recent history of erratic behavior, and it’s not clear Democrats should trust his word. Which means, as tough as the party’s path looks right now, things could always get worse. Which Senate race this year most interests you and why? Let me know at theslatest@slate.com. Election Day is still almost two months away, so let’s transition to the immediate term: how to enjoy your evening. If you’re looking for a comfort watch: Call My Agent!, a delicious comedy series about a French talent agency, is back after several years off the air—and this time, it’s a movie. According to Slate’s Dana Stevens, you’ll enjoy the film even if you haven’t seen the show. But if you do binge the series first, watching it will be like “getting 24 consecutive deliveries of perfectly baked Parisian pastry.” Bon appétit! If you want to get metaphysical: College football is rife with scandals, but the latest one is particularly surreal. Alex Kirshner explains why Michigan’s latest controversy has spectators contemplating the nature of time itself. If you need some enrichment: What’s a “short name for a big explosive” (three letters)? What’s a “gamer cheer sometimes spelled with zeros” (four letters)? Give your brain a workout with today’s crossword. If you’re trying to understand what’s happening online: Raising a kid today requires spending a lot of time navigating digital platforms. There are WhatsApp chats, special apps, YouTube channels, subreddits, Facebook groups, Instagram reels … the chatter, advice, and drama can feel all-consuming. What Next: TBD host Lizzie O’Leary joins forces with ICYMI host Kate Lindsay to make sense of the Wild West that is the parenting internet. OK, time for me to leave you to your own devices. This Trump Speech Sent a Dangerous Message to Iran—and the Rest of the World
2026-09-10

This Trump Speech Sent a Dangerous Message to Iran—and the Rest of the World

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Sometimes I wonder whether President Trump simply doesn’t think before he speaks or whether he has a weird compulsion to blow up the world. It happened again Wednesday during his harangue at the Republican Midterm Convention, which was really a fancy name for a Trump campaign rally. Amid the usual rants of self-puffery and other fictions, he said this: If [Iran] had a nuclear weapon, I’d be calling the Supreme Leader and I’d be saying, ‘Mr. Supreme Leader, how are you, sir? Is there anything we can do for you?’—as opposed to bombing the crap out of him. Many in the audience laughed and applauded, when they should have gasped or moaned. Trump added, “We cannot let them have a nuclear weapon, it’s very simple”—as if that were the lesson of his little tale. But the lesson that any world leader—friend, foe, or in between—would draw from this speech is simpler still: I need to get some nuclear weapons as quickly as possible. Trump is telling the world’s leaders that he might bomb the crap out of them at any moment, or treat them like crap as a whim—but he would kiss the hems of their garments, bestow them with whatever favors they request, if they got themselves some nukes. Maybe this is why he treats Russia’s Vladimir Putin, China’s Xi Jinping, and North Korea’s Kim Jong-un with such respect. Maybe it’s not just that he envies their absolute power on the home front or their unabashedly lavish palaces. Maybe it’s also because, like him, they have enough nuclear bombs to destroy much of life on earth—and he fears they might do so unless he showers them with praise and honor. If this is part of his reasoning, he’s making two mistakes. First, he assumes that they’re as gullible as he is—that, since he believes the compliments they shovel his way, they’ll believe his shovelings too. This almost certainly is not the case; they are much more hardheaded and cynical in their views of the world and their interests. Second, he apparently doesn’t consider what anyone outside the realm of his political base might think. He probably would treat Iran’s leaders differently—certainly he wouldn’t bombard their country with no good reason—if they had a pocketful of nukes to fire back at him. That’s what “nuclear deterrence” is all about; the possession of nuclear weapons deters others from behaving aggressively. But a leader—especially the president of the United States—should know better than to say such things out loud. Doing so flashes a message in bright neon: You want to block me from bombing your country? You want me to be nice? Then go get yourself some nuclear weapons. To the extent Iran still has the technology and the chops to build some A-bombs, you can bet that its scientists are revving up the centrifuges to further enrich their remaining stocks of uranium—while they still can. Kim Jong-un long ago passed this hurdle—which, many have already concluded, is why Iran gets bombed while North Korea’s far more monstrous regime is tolerated, even spoken of respectfully. But to the extent Kim responds to other leaders’ signals, Trump’s comment may move him to accelerate the expansion of Pyongyang’s arsenal. Meanwhile, many of our allies—notably South Korea, Japan, and a few countries in Europe—will no doubt absorb the same message. They have already been thinking about abandoning their restraint when it comes to going nuclear. They have the technical capacity to make the leap, they live in an increasingly dangerous world, they no longer trust the United States to come to their aid in a crisis. And now Trump is loudly confirming the correlation they’ve considered but would rather not think too hard about, until now—that a nuclear arsenal can win them safety and honor from even the most disreputable foes. And when one country goes nuclear, the dominoes start to fall. If Iran builds nukes, Saudi Arabia would certainly follow suit. The royal family has said as much. If South Korea or Japan develops their own deterrent, China will accelerate their own deployments in order to overwhelm their deterrent. Regional arms races worldwide will ensue. There is a school of thought—an extreme branch of “International Realism”—that views nuclear proliferation as a peace-building process: the more nations that have nuclear bombs, the less likely their adversaries will start war. Maybe. But maybe not, even stipulating their premise that all leaders (including savage and adventurous ones) act rationally. New and overly eager nuclear powers might not take the time and care to erect the guardrails that the larger powers gradually installed over the decades—the spy satellites that gauge the size of an opponent’s arsenal, the early-warning sensors that detect an incoming attack, the command-control machinery that ensures only those authorized to push the button have the ability to do so. Even the superpowers’ guardrails—built and maintained at great expense during the Cold War—have failed at times, the radar mistaking flocks of geese for an ICBM attack or a computer mistaking a prerehearsed simulation for the real thing. At least the U.S. and Russia, oceans apart from each other, had half an hour to sort out the confusion before the missiles would land if they had been real. Countries much closer to each other might not have the time. In the real world, as opposed to in academic theory, a world bubbling over with nuclear weapons from many stovetops is more dangerous—not less. Meanwhile, almost every time Trump talks, he sets off another problem, at times to the point of lighting a book of matches or pouring gasoline on a looming fire, often without seeing what he’s doing, without considering that words have consequences. We are learning, more and more, that this is what happens when someone of Trump’s character becomes president. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.
2026-09-10

Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first Republican midterm convention had all the simulacra of a real political convention. There were seats assigned by state and indicated by tall signs on poles, there was a host of precisely timed and deeply forgettable speeches by elected officials, and, of course, there was a keynote speaker. But those were all window dressing to hide what it really was: just another Trump rally. There was news—or at least an eye-popping statement designed to make headlines: Donald Trump promised that, so long as Republicans retained control of both the House and Senate, every American would receive a $5,000 check from the U.S. government. It was a statement perfectly calibrated the next day in newspapers and local news broadcasts. That does not, however, mean it’s likely to happen. Republicans are a long shot to keep the House and far from a lock to hold on to the Senate. There are obvious policy issues with this, as it would cost over a trillion dollars. There are obvious political issues with it: Trump has had issues trying to rally fiscally conservative Republicans behind far less ambitious agenda items. It also was perfectly hedged as a proposal, conditioned only on the scenario that the GOP manages to defy political gravity in November. Still, it’s something new, at a time when Trump’s “Lake America”–style gimmicks have gone stale—a replacement to standard promises from Trump’s first term about building a wall on the U.S.–Mexico border, ending foreign wars, and having so much winning that “your head will spin.” For those more likely to hear about politics from eight-second TikTok clips than 800-word news articles, Trump made masturbation jokes. The speakers preceding him were disjointed and all making their own pitches for why Democrats were bad and Republicans were good. Some touted the merits of last year’s Republican spending bill, now on its third or fourth name ahead of the midterms. Others focused on the evils of Democrats with the remarks of far-left streamer Hasan Piker, who has become a surrogate for a number of Democratic candidates, receiving particular attention. Some were candidates themselves, delivering versions of their own stump speech, just like the array of speakers who proceed Trump at every rally as a way to tide over the restless crowd before the main event. (The most noteworthy speaker before Trump was a recorded video from John Fetterman, who introduced his Republican colleague Dave McCormick. The iconoclastic Pennsylvanian did not back Trump in remarks that seemed designed more to influence Sunday-show bookers than Keystone State swing voters.) And just as there are occasional celebrity cameos at a Trump rally, there was one at the midterm convention, where early-aughts comic Adam Carolla did a routine. When Trump finally took the stage, he spoke for nearly two hours, just like a rally. In the front of the crowd were many of the usual Trump superfans who flock to every rally in the same way that fans of the Grateful Dead used to follow the rock group across the country. The president even shouted them out as he spoke—“the Front-Row Joes” and, of course, “Brick Suit,” a MAGA devotee who wears a suit with a brick pattern on it to show his ardent support for building a border wall with Mexico. The speech itself often felt like a rally speech. It was filled with digressions (Trump took shots at targets ranging from a New York Times writer to Michael Dukakis), and often the president spent an extended stretch as a fainthearted surrogate, urging voters to support Texas Senate nominee Ken Paxton, despite the fact that Paxton dresses badly. But, of course, a mega Trump rally is what the GOP needs right now. Part of the modern role of political conventions is to fix the fractures that emerge within a political party during a presidential primary and signify that everyone is united in a common task. In Trump’s GOP, that’s not an issue. While a handful of moderate dissidents are tacitly tolerated as a matter of expedience, most other heretics have been driven from the tent. The party is united around Trump; the challenge is whether a significant portion of Republicans will vote for anyone besides Trump. The traditional utility of Trump rallies in competitive elections is to energize the MAGA base. At a time when Republican enthusiasm is lagging, that’s what this was about. As Trump told viewers that his wish was for voters to pretend that his name was on the ballot, this was about trying to energize his diehard loyalists—the people who were ready to storm the Capitol on Jan. 6 but didn’t bother to show up in the voting booth in 2022—to turn out in November. Trump rallies have long been effective at this in specific races—Trump’s recent appearance in Myrtle Beach before a primary election in South Carolina may have been enough to steer that race to his preferred candidate—but this was just one big nationalized Trump rally. It might help some, but for anyone hoping it’ll swing the midterms, it probably wouldn’t be prudent to bet an entire $5,000 check. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. BOFH: Oh no! The CMS ate 500 pages of corporate documentation
2026-09-11

BOFH: Oh no! The CMS ate 500 pages of corporate documentation

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
How could such a terrible tragedy have occurred? EPISODE 17 The new Boss is keen to acquire knowledge. So keen that he wants a quick rundown on our content management system so he can avail himself of our copious workplace documentation. "WELL," I say, bracing myself for a long session, "first things first, you'll need to get a strong coffee." "Because our CMS is a place where documents go to die." "Oh, it's bad. So bad that no one will ever admit to being on the selection committee that purchased it." "Were you on it?" the Boss asks pointedly. "What, put an IT person onto a project to select an IT system to store the entirety of our electronic data? Why would you want to do that? No, a selection committee for a project of that importance needs to be made up of people unencumbered by the intellectual weights of reliability, safe data management, and backupability." "You know what I mean. Anyway, a selection committee needs to focus on the big decisions – like the highlight color of tags – and how many different tag colors there are. And whether you can add colors. Then there's the avatar facility." "Yeah, you know, so your avatar appears next to any document you upload. Apparently, it encourages people to contribute." "Oh, it's bad. It doesn't have a proper index, because it uses AI to create tags and keywords dynamically." "Yeah. Though it regenerates those tags every night, based on keyword frequency and lazy theme frequency matching." "Yeah. Say I upload a bunch of documents, each discussing something of vital importance to senior management, like, I dunno, the decline of manila folders. AI runs through overnight and creates tags for manila and folders and then a theme keyword of stationery. "The next day, the PFY slaps in a news item about traffic jams in the Philippines. That night, AI will associate Manila with manila, surmizing it's a typo, associate stationary traffic with stationery for the same reason, and before you know it you're getting a 20-year-old travel expense form when you go to order staples." "It does it every night. And worse still, there's some all-encompassing word association going on inside it which means that if you delete the document, AI will retain the keyword and try and match it to the next 'best' document." "Why would I want to stop that happening?" "No one wants us to fix it. Remember when I said it's where documents go to die?" "The staff love it. Say you've half-completed a report that was due a couple of weeks ago, and you're under the pump to deliver it. Just put it in the CMS and that's the end of the matter. No one's going to look for it." "At my former workplace we used to use ShareP-" "Don't say that word. Or the T word. It's worse than Candyman." "Well, they have the 'prior art' when it comes to sending documents into Neverland. No, we're happy with our system, which is almost as good as one of those shredders that slices the paper up before chopping it up into confetti." "I don't think that senior management would accept that sort of syst-" "Senior management were the ones who asked for it! We make the system available to His Majesty's Revenue and Customs on a regular basis. It takes about three weeks to convert a forensic accountant into a mindless ghost watering invisible plants in a mental health facility." "It can't be that bad. I mean, why would we keep it – at a functional level, I mean?" "No one knows. The original invoice is in the CMS." "We could, but you're pretty much guaranteed never to get served the same document twice." "Well, you know. There was a negotiation process. Lots of letters of engagement, counter-letters of engagement, example contract, pre-draft, draft, pre-pre-signing, pre-signing, amended, cross-amended, legally reviewed, counter-legally reviewed, signed in principle, signed, countersigned, amended countersigned, etc." "You said amended countersigned... but you didn't say amended signed." "No, I didn't, did I? Maybe that's not in the system, but then again, maybe it is. After you've downloaded 17 documents – all slightly different, some dated, some undated, definitely NOT added in chronological order – you'll start to feel like you're getting somewhere. Then you'll get the 18th document, which refers to another document you've never heard of, which supersedes all the documents thus far." "And I think you'll find I don't have to find it for you. It's in my contract." "And don't tell me, your contract is in the CMS?" On Call: Boss wrongly claimed IT deleted his files, refused to learn the one-click fix

On Call: Boss wrongly claimed IT deleted his files, refused to learn the one-click fix
